> AGENT

[REDACTED]’s last message was at **2026-05-14 09:37:30 Berlin**: “So you don’t really know how to do that?”

The bot did generate a reply and inserted it at **09:38:07**, but Discord rejected the send:

`400 Bad Request` posting to Discord channel `1499561548237242398`.

The likely direct cause is length: the generated reply was **2,118 characters**, over Discord’s **2,000 character** message limit. The row is therefore marked `processing_state='expired'` and has no provider message id, so [REDACTED] never saw it.

There’s also a logging bug: `turn_audit_events` says `outbound.sent`, but that only means the app created/attempted the outbound row. The actual provider send failed afterward. The code path should split or clamp final Discord replies before sending, and it should audit provider failures distinctly.

> DEVELOPER

Ahhh, so how can we avvoid such issues in the future?

> AGENT

Use defense in depth, because relying on the model to stay under transport limits will fail occasionally.

1. **Hard limit at the transport boundary**
   Before any Discord send, validate `len(content) <= 2000`. If not, split into multiple messages or fail before creating a misleading “sent” row.

2. **Split final replies, not just `send_message_part`**
   The app already has incremental Discord parts, but this failure came through the normal final `send_outbound()` path. That path needs the same Discord-aware splitting/clamping behavior.

3. **Mark provider failures honestly**
   `turn_audit_events` currently recorded `outbound.sent` even though Discord rejected it. Rename/split the audit states:
   - `outbound.created`
   - `provider.send.started`
   - `provider.send.succeeded`
   - `provider.send.failed`

4. **Store the Discord error body**
   Right now the row only says `400 Bad Request`. Discord usually returns a JSON body explaining the exact validation error. Log that into turn reasoning/audit metadata.

5. **Add tests**
   Add a regression test where a 2,001+ character Discord reply is generated and assert:
   - it does not silently expire as one message,
   - it is split into valid Discord-sized chunks, or withheld with a clear reason,
   - the audit trail records provider failure correctly if Discord rejects it.

The cleanest fix is probably: add a Discord message splitter in `app/services/messaging.py`, use it in `send_outbound()` for provider `discord`, and only mark `final_output_message_id` after at least one provider send succeeds.

> DEVELOPER

Could we split them up at sensible boundaries? Like the end of a paragraph if >1000 characters? What's teh point at which to do that?

> AGENT

Yes. I’d split Discord outbound on semantic boundaries, with conservative thresholds well below 2,000.

A good policy:

- **Target chunk size:** ~1,000-1,300 chars
- **Hard max:** 1,900 chars, leaving margin for Discord quirks and future prefixes
- **Preferred boundaries, in order:**
  1. Blank line / paragraph break
  2. Sentence end: `.`, `?`, `!`
  3. Clause break: `;`, `:`, em dash/comma only if needed
  4. Word boundary fallback
  5. Hard character split only as last resort

I would not split only once the text exceeds 2,000. At that point the content is already shaped as one long message. Instead:

- If final reply is **≤ 1,400 chars**, send as one message.
- If final reply is **> 1,400 chars**, split at paragraphs/sentences into multiple Discord messages.
- Ensure every chunk is **≤ 1,900 chars** no matter what.

For [REDACTED]’s failed reply, the 2,118-char message should probably have become 2 messages: first the acknowledgement/framing, second the concrete suggestions. That would feel natural in chat and avoid the Discord hard limit.

I’d also keep model prompting as a soft preference, but enforce this in code. The transport layer should be the final authority.

> DEVELOPER

let's prioritsie line breaks/paragraph breaks - is this hard to do?

> AGENT

It’s not hard. I’m going to make it transport-layer behavior: Discord final replies get split before provider send, preferring paragraph breaks first, then sentence/word fallback so no chunk crosses the hard limit.

> AGENT

I’m keeping the first patch small: a pure splitter helper plus Discord-only use in the final outbound path. It won’t touch the pending unrelated edits already in the worktree.

part.strip()]\n+    for paragraph in paragraphs:\n+        paragraph_chunks = _split_discord_paragraph(paragraph)\n+        for paragraph_chunk in paragraph_chunks:\n+            candidate = f\"{current}\\n\\n{paragraph_chunk}\" if current else paragraph_chunk\n+            if current and len(candidate) > _DISCORD_SPLIT_TARGET_CHARS:\n+                chunks.append(current)\n+                current = paragraph_chunk\n+            else:\n+                current = candidate\n+    if current:\n+        chunks.append(current)\n+    return chunks\n+\n+\n+def _split_discord_paragraph(paragraph: str) -> list[str]:\n+    if len(paragraph) <= _DISCORD_HARD_MAX_CHARS:\n+        return [paragraph]\n+\n+    chunks: list[str] = []\n+    remaining = paragraph.strip()\n+    while len(remaining) > _DISCORD_HARD_MAX_CHARS:\n+        split_at = _discord_split_index(remaining, _DISCORD_HARD_MAX_CHARS)\n+        chunks.append(remaining[:split_at].strip())\n+        remaining = remaining[split_at:].strip()\n+    if remaining:\n+        chunks.append(remaining)\n+    return chunks\n+\n+\n+def _discord_split_index(text: str, limit: int) -> int:\n+    window = text[:limit]\n+    floor = max(limit // 2, 1)\n+    sentence_breaks = [\n+        window.rfind(\". \"),\n+        window.rfind(\"? \"),\n+        window.rfind(\"! \"),\n+    ]\n+    sentence_at = max(sentence_breaks)\n+    if sentence_at >= floor:\n+        return sentence_at + 1\n+\n+    word_at = window.rfind(\" \")\n+    if word_at >= floor:\n+        return word_at\n+    return limit\n*** End Patch\n"
```json
}
```

> AGENT

Implemented. It’s a small transport-layer change in [app/services/messaging.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/messaging.py): Discord final replies now split before sending, targeting `1000` chars and never exceeding `1900`. It prioritizes paragraph breaks, then sentence breaks, then word boundaries, then hard splits only as a last resort.

I added a regression test in [tests/test_send_outbound.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/tests/test_send_outbound.py) that verifies a long multi-paragraph Discord reply is sent as separate messages and only shows typing on the first chunk.

Verified:
`20 passed in 0.17s` via `.venv/bin/pytest tests/test_send_outbound.py -q`

I also ran `py_compile` successfully. `ruff` wasn’t runnable through `uv` because this shell’s pyenv points at missing Python `3.8.10`, but the targeted test suite passes.

pool.fetchrow(\n            '''\n            SELECT m.content, m.bot_id, m.topic_id, b.user_in_context, te.metadata\n            FROM messages m\n            JOIN bot_turns b ON b.id = m.bot_turn_id\n            LEFT JOIN turn_audit_events te ON te.turn_id = b.id AND te.event_type = 'turn.opened'\n            WHERE m.id = $1::uuid\n            ORDER BY te.event_seq NULLS LAST\n            LIMIT 1\n channel_id=metadata.get('channel_id'),\n            binding_id=metadata.get('binding_id') or uuid4(),\n            dyad_id=metadata.get('dyad_id') or uuid4(),\n        )\n        new_final_id = await Yes: Hector has actually executed.

Production evidence:

- Hector bot row exists: `hector / Hector`
- Binding exists for Peter: `bot_id=hector`, `user_id=faed044b...`
- He has run **9 turns today**, from **13:35 to 14:44 Berlin**, all completed, no failures.
- He is sending/receiving Discord messages under `bot_id='hector'`.
- Turns are using `llm_provider=deepseek`, `model_version=deepseek-chat`.

But: he is **not really using the tool substrate yet**.

What I found:

- `tool_calls`: only **1** Hector tool call total, and it was just `send_message_part`.
- `memories` recorded by Hector: **0**
- `observations` recorded by Hector: **0**
- `themes` recorded by Hector: **0**
- `commitments` for Hector: **0**
- `events` for Hector: **0**

The latest turn did run through `read -> respond -> record -> schedule`, but both `record` and `schedule` completed with `tool_call_count=0`. So when Hector said something like “let’s put this on the board,” he did **not** actually call `create_commitment`, `log_event`, `add_memory`, etc.

One extra weirdness: the first inbound `"Bey"` triggered **two Hector turns** almost simultaneously, one with a binding and one with `binding_id=null`. That looks like a duplicate path/catch-up issue worth checking separately.

> AGENT

Yes. I looked through both the production records and Hector’s prompt/tool setup. The issue is not that the DB write tools are missing. It’s that Hector is under-instructed and the turn planner often bypasses the write phase.

What I found:

- Hector **can** see memory/observation tools. His allowlist includes `get_memories`, `add_memory`, `get_observations`, `log_observation`, `list_commitments`, `create_commitment`, `log_event`, etc.
- But his per-step instructions are still literal stubs: [app/bots/hector.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/bots/hector.py:52)
  ```python
  "record": "Record step (Hector phase 1 stub)."
  ```
- His persona prompt only gives explicit write instructions for:
  - concrete plans -> `create_commitment`
  - adherence reports -> `log_event`

  See [app/bots/prompts/hector.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/bots/prompts/hector.py:162).

It does **not** clearly say: “save durable fitness constraints, routines, obstacles, preferred timing, equipment setup, etc. as memories/observations.”

The other big factor: most Hector turns used `quick_reply`, which is only `respond -> done`, so there is no `record` step at all. That comes from [app/services/turn_plan.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/turn_plan.py:13). Unless the message looks like an explicit memory request, audit request, charged message, etc., the runner defaults to quick reply.

For the one later “standard” turn that did include `read -> respond -> record -> schedule`, the record step ran but made zero tool calls. That’s likely because the model had only the generic/stub “Record step” instruction, and because writes require prior reads: e.g. `create_commitment` requires `list_commitments`, `add_memory` requires `get_memories`, `log_observation` requires `get_observations` per [app/services/tools/registry.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/tools/registry.py:377).

So the diagnosis is:

1. Hector is replying conversationally.
2. Hector has the tools available.
3. Hector has not been given strong enough policy for what fitness context to persist.
4. Many turns skip `record` entirely.
5. Even when `record` runs, the stub step instruction gives him no good push to read-before-write and persist.

So yes, from your conversation, there were obvious things he probably should have saved: morning preference, work absorption later in the day, bench/computer room friction, dog walk constraint, laptop-before-workout gate, and possibly a concrete weekday workout commitment. He just wasn’t prompted/planned strongly enough to do it.

> AGENT

I’ll compare Hector against the mediator/Véas bot prompt and step instructions, then I’ll give a concrete recommendation. I’m looking specifically for durable-state guidance: what to save, when to read-before-write, and how the turn plan gets a record phase.

> AGENT

Yes. Véas has much stronger “save useful context” instructions than Hector.

Véas has:

- A full “Six Knowledge Primitives” section: style notes, memories, themes, watch items, observations, distillations.
- Clear discriminator rules like “specific fact -> memory” and “learned pattern -> observation.”
- A record-step instruction that explicitly says to record “memories, observations, distillations, theme updates, watch items...” when justified.
- A global “Search before writing” rule.
- Non-stub step instructions.

Hector currently has:

- Good persona instructions.
- Good commitment/event instructions.
- But stub step instructions: `Record step (Hector phase 1 stub).`
- No Hector-specific “fitness knowledge primitives” section.
- No explicit guidance that useful fitness context should be saved as memory/observation unless it becomes a formal commitment/event.

So my read is: **this is mostly instruction/planning, not reasoning horsepower.**

Higher reasoning might help a bit, and DeepSeek may be less reliable at proactive tool use than Claude, but the bigger issue is that Hector has not been told clearly enough what counts as durable fitness context, and most turns are classified as `quick_reply`, which skips `record` entirely.

What I’d do:

1. **Give Hector real step instructions**
   Replace the phase-1 stubs with mediator-style instructions. Especially record:

   “Record durable fitness context: constraints, preferred workout windows, equipment/location setup, recurring blockers, successful tactics, concrete plans, adherence reports, and user preferences. Use memory for facts/constraints, observation for patterns, commitment for agreed tracked plans, event for completed/missed/excused sessions.”

2. **Add Hector-specific knowledge primitives**
   Not all of Véas’s relationship primitives apply. For Hector:
   - **Memory:** concrete facts/constraints: “bench is in same room as computer,” “walks dog with wife in the morning,” “work absorbs him later in day.”
   - **Observation:** recurring pattern: “later-day workouts get displaced by work absorption.”
   - **Commitment:** explicit trackable plan: “weekday workout before laptop.”
   - **Event:** “did/missed/excused today’s workout.”
   - **Watch item/task:** “check next week whether laptop-before-workout ritual held.”

3. **Adjust turn planning for fitness context**
   Messages about routine, blockers, plans, equipment, schedule, adherence, or “what should I do next week” should use `standard`, not `quick_reply`. Otherwise Hector never reaches record.

4. **Force read-before-write ergonomically**
   Since writes require reads, Hector’s read step should proactively call:
   - `list_commitments` when plans/adherence are discussed
   - `get_memories` for durable facts/constraints
   - `get_observations` for recurring blockers/patterns

5. **Only then consider model upgrade**
   If after better prompt/planning he still skips writes, move Hector record steps to a stronger model or stricter tool-choice path. But I would not start there.

For the conversation you had, I’d expect Hector to save at least:
- memory: morning dog walk with wife is part of routine
- memory: workout bench is near computer/work setup
- observation: work absorption later in day crowds out workouts
- memory/observation: moving laptop away at night may support morning workout
- commitment, if you explicitly agreed: weekday workout before laptop / 20 min minimum.

> DEVELOPER

And does VEA have a phase by phase kind of instruction? So for each particular step, you know, just do different things. And when adding memories, does it first search up in both cases, if they first search up existing memories to try to find existing ones, you know, an observation to update instead of just creating duplicates or how does that work?

> AGENT

Yes. Véas has both global phase guidance and per-step instructions.

The per-step instructions live in [app/bots/mediator.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/bots/mediator.py:45):

- `read`: gather only needed context; no user text, no writes.
- `consult`: only use second opinion if explicitly asked.
- `respond`: produce user-facing response/reaction/silence.
- `record`: record durable state: memories, observations, distillations, theme updates, watch items, bridge candidates, style notes, feedback, OOB updates.
- `schedule`: optionally schedule useful follow-up.
- `done`: stop.

And yes, Véas is explicitly told to search before writing. In [app/services/prompts.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/prompts.py:288), the global rule says to read with `get_*`, `list_*`, or `search_*` before adding/updating/revising/superseding memory, observation, distillation, theme, watch item, OOB, or style notes, and to prefer update/reinforce/revise over creating a new row.

There is also an enforcement layer in [app/services/tools/registry.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/tools/registry.py:377):

```python
"add_memory": {"get_memories"},
"update_memory": {"get_memories"},
"log_observation": {"get_observations"},
"update_observation": {"get_observations"},
"create_theme": {"list_themes", "get_theme"},
"update_theme": {"list_themes", "get_theme"},
```

So if the model tries to call `add_memory` before `get_memories`, the dispatcher should reject it. Same for observations/themes. That is why the intended pattern is:

1. `read` step: call `get_memories` / `get_observations` / `list_themes`.
2. `respond` step: answer the user.
3. `record` step: call `add_memory` or `update_memory`, `log_observation` or `update_observation`, etc.

Hector has access to the same machinery, but his own step instructions are currently stubs, so he isn’t being steered into that pattern reliably.

Save useful future context even when it is not dramatic.\n+\n+- Memories are stable concrete facts: schedule constraints, equipment,\n+  preferred training windows, family/support setup, recurring logistics,\n+  and strong preferences. Example: the bench is near the computer, or the\n+  user protects a dog walk with their wife.\n+- Observations are patterns and tactics: what tends to derail the user,\n+  what timing works, what kind of plan survives work pressure, and what\n+  seems to make adherence easier. Example: once work starts, later workouts\n+  often get crowded out.\n+- Commitments are explicit concrete plans the user has agreed to track:\n+  named days, minimum dose, time window, or scope. Do not turn vague intent\n+  into a commitment.\n+- Events are adherence reports against commitments: completed, missed, or\n+  excused slots. Keep the board honest without moralizing.\n+- Follow-ups or scheduled tasks are for genuinely useful future nudges,\n+  reviews, or check-ins, not for every casual mention.\n+\n+A single message can justify more than one durable update. Changes made:

- Added a succinct “Fitness Knowledge Primitives” section to [app/bots/prompts/hector.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/bots/prompts/hector.py:162): memories, observations, commitments, events, follow-ups, read-before-write, duplicate avoidance, and safety boundaries.
- Updated turn planning in [app/services/turn_plan.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/turn_plan.py:60) so Hector fitness-context messages like plans, routines, missed/done slots, laptop/bench/workout logistics, etc. use the `standard` path with `record`.
- Passed `bot_id` / topic into planner signals in [app/services/agentic.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/Veas/app/services/agentic.py:1007), so this behavior is Hector-specific.
- Added focused tests in `tests/test_turn_plan.py` and `tests/test_hector_prompt.py`.

Verification:
- `42 passed` for:
  - `tests/test_hector_prompt.py`
  - `tests/test_turn_plan.py`
  - `tests/test_hector_step_phase.py`
- `py_compile` passed for the edited modules.

If we want stricter behavior later, the next step would be a post-turn “should durable state have been written?” auditor/classifier.

> DEVELOPER

Does Tante Rosi have a similar approach?

> AGENT

I’ll check Tante Rosi’s bot spec, prompt, and step instructions against the Hector/Véas pattern so I can answer concretely rather than guessing from memory.

> AGENT

Not really. Tante Rosi has the same underlying machinery, but not the same engineered prompting/planning approach.

What she has:
- Same enforced phase/tool system as Hector and Véas.
- Pregnancy-specific tools in her allowlist: `set_pregnancy_edd`, `correct_pregnancy_edd`, `end_pregnancy`.
- Prompt guidance saying to use those tools when the user gives state-changing pregnancy info.
- Access to general memory/observation tools through the normal record phase.

What she does **not** currently have:
- Her BotSpec phase instructions are still stubs: `Read step (Rosi phase 1 stub)`, etc. in `app/bots/tante_rosi.py`.
- No pregnancy equivalent of Hector’s new “Knowledge Primitives” section.
- No Rosi-specific planner routing. So a normal message like “my scan moved the due date” may still get `quick_reply` unless another rule promotes it to `standard`, which means she may never reach the `record` phase where pregnancy tools are available.
- No read-before-write rule for pregnancy tools specifically. The pregnancy handlers do state checks, but the planner/prompt does not force a prior read the same way memory/observation/commitment writes do.

So the accurate answer is: Tante Rosi is partially set up, but she has the same likely failure class Hector had. The prompt says “use the pregnancy tools,” but the per-phase instructions and planner do not strongly ensure she gets to the phase where she can use them.

I’d recommend doing the same treatment for her:
- Replace Rosi’s phase stubs with pregnancy-specific read/respond/record/schedule instructions.
- Add a compact “Pregnancy Knowledge Primitives” section.
- Add Rosi-specific planner promotion for due date, scan correction, pregnancy ended/birth/loss/termination, appointment logistics, symptoms, support needs, and partner-share choices.
- Add tests that those messages route to `standard`, while casual greetings stay quick.

Don't push if they deflect.\n+\n+# Pregnancy Knowledge Primitives\n+\n+Use durable state so you can remember the pregnancy context that actually\n+matters later. Save useful future context even when it is ordinary.\n+\n+- Pregnancy state is the formal pregnancy timeline: confirmed EDD, dating\n+  basis, scan correction, birth, loss, or termination. Only update this\n+  when the user explicitly gives the state change. Do not infer.\n+- Memories are stable concrete facts: appointment logistics, support setup,\n+  broad preferences, non-sensitive milestones, practical constraints, and\n+  pregnancy facts the user treats as ordinary context.\n+- Observations are patterns and support signals: recurring worries, what\n+  helps the user feel steadier, what tends to overwhelm them, and repeated\n+  practical needs.\n+- Open asks are missing pregnancy setup facts you should gently collect\n+  when there is room, one per turn.\n+- Follow-ups or scheduled tasks are for genuinely useful future nudges:\n+  after scans, appointments, difficult symptom conversations, open asks, or\n+  explicit reminders. They are not for every casual mention.\n+\n+A single message can justify more than one durable update. For example,\n+\"the scan moved the due date and I'm nervous about telling my partner\" may\n+correct the EDD and also preserve an observation about scan-related anxiety\n+or a memory about support needs.\n+\n+Before adding or updating durable state, read existing memories,\n+observations, or pregnancy state first and update/reinforce the existing row\n+when that is cleaner than creating a duplicate.\n+\n+Keep sensitive medical details, symptoms, loss history, fears, sexual\n+details, and relationship conflict private unless the user explicitly asks\n+to share a specific safe version. Do not save diagnoses or clinical\n+conclusions.\n {scheduling_section}{partner_nudge_section}{partner_sharing_section}\n - One question per reply, maximum. Don't interview.\n - Keep replies short by default. Longer only when there's substance to\n*** End Patch\n"

First read \"\n+            \"the adherence board with get_adherence and recent events if \"\n+            \"needed. If the relevant slot is already done, missed, or excused, \"\n+            \"stay silent unless a short acknowledgement is clearly useful. If the commitment \"\n+            \"is no longer active, cancel this current scheduled task.\"\n+        ),\n+    }\n+    try:\n+        await ctx.pool.fetchrow(\n+            \"\"\"\n+            INSERT INTO scheduled_jobs (user_id, job_type, scheduled_for, context, status, bot_id, topic_id)\n+            SELECT $1, 'scheduled_task', $2, $3::jsonb, 'pending', $4, $5\n+            WHERE NOT EXISTS (\n+                SELECT 1\n+                FROM scheduled_jobs\n+                WHERE user_id = $1\n+                  AND job_type = 'scheduled_task'\n+                  AND status = 'pending'\n+                  AND bot_id = $4\n+                  AND topic_id = $5\n+                  AND context->>'kind' = 'commitment_checkin'\n+                  AND context->>'commitment_id' = $6\n+            )\n+            RETURNING id AS job_id, scheduled_for, context\n+            \"\"\",\n+            ctx.user.id,\n+            scheduled_for,\n+            context,\n+            ctx.bot_id,\n+            ctx.primary_topic_id,\n+            commitment_id,\n+        )\n+    except Exception:\n+        logger.exception(\n+            \"failed to schedule commitment check-in\",\n+            extra=obs_fields(ctx),\n+        )\n+\n+\n+def _commitment_checkin_schedule(\n+    ctx: TurnContext,\n+    args: \"CreateCommitmentInput\",\n+) -> tuple[datetime | None, dict[str, Any] | None]:\n+    timezone = _commitment_timezone(ctx, args)\n+    now_local = datetime.now(UTC).astimezone(timezone)\n+    start = date.fromisoformat(args.start_date) if args.start_date else now_local.date()\n+    end = date.fromisoformat(args.end_date) if args.end_date else None\n+\n+    if args.cadence == \"daily\":\n+        weekdays = None\n+        local_time = datetime.min.time().replace(hour=20, minute=30)\n+        recurrence: dict[str, Any] = {\"type\": \"daily\", \"interval\": 1}\n+    elif args.cadence == \"weekdays\":\n+        weekdays = [0, 1, 2, 3, 4]\n+        local_time = datetime.min.time().replace(hour=20, minute=30)\n+        recurrence = {\"type\": \"weekly\", \"interval\": 1, \"weekdays\": weekdays}\n+    elif args.cadence == \"custom_days\":\n+        weekdays = list(args.days_of_week or [])\n+        local_time = datetime.min.time().replace(hour=20, minute=30)\n+        recurrence = {\"type\": \"weekly\", \"interval\": 1, \"weekdays\": weekdays}\n+    elif args.cadence in {\"weekly_count\", \"custom\"}:\n+        weekdays = [6]\n+        local_time = datetime.min.time().replace(hour=19, minute=0)\n+        recurrence = {\"type\": \"weekly\", \"interval\": 1, \"weekdays\": weekdays}\n+    else:\n+        return None, None\n+\n+    if end is not None:\n+        recurrence[\"until\"] = datetime.combine(\n+            end,\n+            datetime.max.time().replace(microsecond=0),\n+            tzinfo=timezone,\n+        ).astimezone(UTC).isoformat()\n+\n+    first = _next_local_commitment_checkin(\n+        now_local=now_local,\n+        start=start,\n+        local_time=local_time,\n+        weekdays=weekdays,\n+    )\n+    if end is not None and first.date() > end:\n+        return None, None\n+    return first.astimezone(UTC), recurrence\n+\n+\n+def _commitment_timezone(ctx: TurnContext, args: \"CreateCommitmentInput\") -> ZoneInfo:\n+    timezone_name = None\n+    if args.schedule_rule is not None:\n+        timezone_name = args.schedule_rule.timezone\n+    timezone_name = timezone_name or getattr(ctx.user, \"timezone\", None) or \"UTC\"\n+    try:\n+        return ZoneInfo(timezone_name)\n+    except ZoneInfoNotFoundError:\n+        return ZoneInfo(\"UTC\")\n+\n+\n+def _next_local_commitment_checkin(\n+    *,\n+    now_local: datetime,\n+    start: date,\n+    local_time: Any,\n+    weekdays: list[int] | None,\n+) -> datetime:\n+    candidate_date = max(now_local.date(), start)\n+    for offset in range(0, 15):\n+        day = candidate_date + timedelta(days=offset)\n+        if weekdays is not None and day.weekday() not in weekdays:\n+            continue\n+        candidate = datetime.combine(day, local_time, tzinfo=now_local.tzinfo)\n+        if candidate > now_local:\n+            return candidate\n+    return datetime.combine(\n+        candidate_date + timedelta(days=1),\n+        local_time,\n+        tzinfo=now_local.tzinfo,\n+    )\n+\n+\n+async def _cancel_commitment_checkin_tasks(\n+    ctx: TurnContext,\n+    commitment_id: str,\n+) -> None:\n+    try:\n+        await ctx.pool.execute(\n+            \"\"\"\n+            UPDATE scheduled_jobs\n+            SET status = 'cancelled',\n+                cancellation_reason = 'commitment_closed',\n+                updated_at = now()\n+            WHERE user_id = $1\n+              AND bot_id = $2\n+              AND topic_id = $3\n+              AND job_type = 'scheduled_task'\n+              AND status = 'pending'\n+              AND context->>'kind' = 'commitment_checkin'\n+              AND context->>'commitment_id' = $4\n+            \"\"\",\n+            ctx.user.id,\n+            ctx.bot_id,\n+            ctx.primary_topic_id,\n+            str(commitment_id),\n+        )\n+    except Exception:\n+        logger.exception(\n+            \"failed to cancel commitment check-in tasks\",\n+            extra=obs_fields(ctx),\n+        )\n*** End Patch\n"
```json
}
```

> AGENT

Implemented both pieces.

For Tante Rosi:
- Replaced her phase stubs with real pregnancy-specific `read/respond/record/schedule` instructions in `app/bots/tante_rosi.py`.
- Added “Pregnancy Knowledge Primitives” to `app/bots/prompts/tante_rosi.py`.
- Added Rosi-specific planner routing in `app/services/turn_plan.py`, so pregnancy state messages like due date changes, scans, appointments, birth/loss, symptoms, etc. go through `standard` and reach `record`.
- Added tests covering the prompt, phase instructions, and planner behavior.

For Hector commitment reminders:
- `create_commitment` now automatically creates a recurring internal `scheduled_task`.
- The scheduled task tells Hector to check `get_adherence` first.
- If the slot is already `done`, `missed`, or `excused`, he should stay quiet unless a small acknowledgement is clearly useful.
- If it is still unknown after the intended window, he asks one low-key check-in question.
- `close_commitment` now cancels pending check-in tasks for that commitment.
- Defaults:
  - `daily`: every evening at 20:30 local time.
  - `weekdays`: Mon-Fri at 20:30 local time.
  - `custom_days`: those days at 20:30 local time.
  - `weekly_count` / `custom`: Sunday at 19:00 local time.

Verification:
- `99 passed` for the focused Rosi/Hector/planner suite.
- `63 passed` for related allowlist/schema/persona tests.
- `py_compile` passed for the edited modules.

> DEVELOPER

And say if it's like a daily commitment, does that mean that it has a daily scheduled task?

> AGENT

Yes.

For a `daily` Hector commitment, `create_commitment` now creates one recurring `scheduled_task` with:

- first fire: next local 20:30
- recurrence: daily, interval 1
- scope: same user, `bot_id="hector"`, fitness topic
- brief: check adherence first, stay quiet if already handled, ask one low-key question only if the slot is still unknown

It is not many separate jobs upfront. It is one pending recurring scheduled task. When it fires, the scheduler creates the next occurrence from the recurrence rule.

> DEVELOPER

And does it say that it doesn't necessarily have to reply and does the task get scheduled even if the commitment has been fulfilled?

> AGENT

Yes on the first part: the scheduled task brief explicitly says to check adherence first and “stay silent” if the relevant slot is already `done`, `missed`, or `excused`, unless a short acknowledgement is clearly useful.

On the second part: the task is scheduled when the commitment is created, regardless of whether a future slot later gets fulfilled. It does **not** pre-cancel itself when the user logs the workout earlier in the day. Instead, when the scheduled task fires, Hector checks adherence at that moment and should stay silent if it’s already fulfilled.

So the mechanism is: schedule always for active commitments, suppress at execution time if already handled.

> DEVELOPER

Cool. And can you see why Hector didn't reply to me there?

> AGENT

Yes. The missed reply was intentional suppression, not a crash.

For the message:

`regrouping for next week!`

Hector opened a turn at **May 15, 2026 11:42:16 UTC**. While that reply was being prepared/sent, you sent a newer message:

`Want to have and execute a plan taht I can nail`

at **11:42:21 UTC**.

The first turn then logged:

`outbound.withheld`  
reason: `newer_inbound_during_send`

So the system skipped the first outbound to avoid replying to stale context after your next message arrived. Then Hector handled the newer message in a second turn and sent:

`Alright, regrouping for next week — I like that...`

at **11:42:48 UTC**.

So: he did not ignore you; the anti-stale-reply guard withheld the earlier response and let the newer combined context win.

> AGENT

You were right. That message exists in Discord, but it never reached the app.

Timeline:

- **13:12:21.640 UTC**: Hector’s Discord gateway disconnected:
  `WS closed code=1006`
- **13:12:22.159 UTC**: you sent the long weightlifting/running plan message.
- **13:12:25.338 UTC**: Hector reconnected and got `READY`.
- There is **no `MESSAGE_CREATE` log** for your message.
- There is **no `messages` DB row** for it.
- There is **no Hector turn** after it.

So Hector didn’t reply because the message landed during a tiny Discord websocket disconnect window. The app reconnects, but it currently only runs Discord catch-up on startup, not after reconnects, so that missed message was never backfilled.

Fix I’d recommend: after every gateway reconnect/`READY`, run `catch_up_recent_messages(...)` for that bot. That would have pulled this message from Discord and processed it.

> TOOL

tool_use exec_command

> DEVELOPER

Woudl taht apply to every bot? What's the well-engineered solution here?

> AGENT

Yes, this can apply to every Discord bot: mediator, Tante Rosi, Hector, and any future per-bot Discord gateway.

The well-engineered fix is two-layered:

1. **Reconnect catch-up for every bot**
   After a gateway reconnects and receives `READY`/`RESUMED`, run a bounded catch-up for that specific bot’s DM channel. That catches messages sent during the websocket gap.

2. **Make catch-up bot-scoped**
   Current catch-up should not use “latest message from this Discord user across all bots” as the cursor. It should use the latest inbound message for **that bot**. Otherwise a newer Tante Rosi or mediator message could cause Hector catch-up to skip older Hector-channel messages.

Best version:
- Each Discord gateway owns its own bot token, channel, coalescer, and catch-up.
- On startup: catch up recent messages for that bot.
- On reconnect: catch up recent messages for that bot.
- Cursor query filters by `bot_id`.
- Processing stays idempotent by Discord message id, so replaying a small window is safe.
- Longer-term: support Discord `RESUME` using session id + sequence number, but still keep REST catch-up as the safety net because reconnects can still lose edge events.

So the answer is: yes, generalize it for all bots, but make it per-bot and idempotent. The specific bug here was not “Hector chose not to reply”; it was “Hector’s gateway dropped for a few seconds and no reconnect catch-up ran.”

> AGENT

Yes. The right generalized approach is to treat inbound Discord messages as a durable queue, not as “gateway event arrives, immediately answer, hope nothing breaks.”

I’d do it like this:

**1. Store every inbound message idempotently**
Use Discord message id as the external id. In this app that is currently `messages.whatsapp_message_id`, despite the name.

Add/ensure a unique constraint like:

```sql
UNIQUE (bot_id, transport, whatsapp_message_id)
```

or, with current columns:

```sql
UNIQUE (bot_id, whatsapp_message_id)
WHERE whatsapp_message_id IS NOT NULL
```

Then catch-up can safely replay recent Discord history. If the row already exists, do nothing or update harmless metadata. No duplicate processing.

**2. Separate “ingested” from “handled”**
Right now `processing_state` is doing some of this, but we should make the semantics crisp.

Suggested states:

```text
raw          message stored, not yet claimed
processing   a worker/turn is currently handling it
processed    successfully included in a completed turn
deferred     intentionally waiting/coalescing
expired      intentionally no longer needs direct handling
failed       attempted but errored and should be retryable/inspectable
```

No separate `addressed` column is necessary if `processing_state` is reliable. If we want clearer semantics, add `handled_at`, `handled_by_turn_id`, and maybe `processing_error`.

**3. Catch-up should always be safe to run**
On startup and reconnect, for each bot:

- fetch last N Discord messages from that bot’s DM channel
- insert any missing inbound messages with `processing_state='raw'`
- for existing inbound messages, check whether they are fully handled
- trigger processing for any message in `raw` or retryable `failed`
- leave `processed` alone
- leave `deferred` alone unless it is stale

That handles:
- websocket disconnects
- app downtime
- deploy restarts
- process crashes after storing but before replying
- transient model/tool/send failures

**4. Use bot-scoped cursors, but prefer replay windows**
A strict “last seen id” cursor can still skip things if it is wrong. Better:

- fetch the last 50 or 100 messages every reconnect/startup
- idempotently upsert them
- process unhandled ones

This is simpler and safer than relying only on a cursor.

**5. Add a stale-message sweeper**
A background job should periodically find messages like:

```sql
direction='inbound'
AND processing_state IN ('raw', 'failed')
AND sent_at > now() - interval '7 days'
```

and enqueue/process them.

Also recover stuck messages:

```sql
processing_state='processing'
AND updated_at < now() - interval '5 minutes'
```

Those should move back to `raw` or `failed`.

**6. Mark completion only after the turn really finishes**
A message should become `processed` only after a bot turn completes successfully or deliberately withholds/silences it. Ideally store:

```text
handled_by_turn_id
handled_at
handling_result = replied | silent | withheld_newer_inbound | no_action | failed
```

That gives you auditability without guessing.

For this codebase, I’d implement the first version as:

- make `catch_up_recent_messages` bot-scoped
- run catch-up after every gateway `READY` reconnect, not just startup
- change catch-up to replay a bounded recent window, not only `after=last_seen`
- upsert by `(bot_id, whatsapp_message_id)`
- after catch-up, process any unprocessed inbound rows for that bot/channel
- add tests for duplicate replay and missed reconnect message recovery

That is the robust shape. No duplicate replies, and missed messages get recovered automatically.

$megaplan-rubric

> AGENT

For this message-recovery work, I’d classify it as:

`premium/standard/medium`

Why:
- **Tier:** `premium` because this touches production message ingestion, duplicate-prevention, retry semantics, and likely a DB constraint/migration. A bad fix can cause duplicate replies or missed user messages.
- **Robustness:** `standard`, not `robust`, unless we also do a full queue/sweeper/state-machine migration in the same sprint. Standard is enough for a scoped reconnect catch-up + idempotency fix with tests.
- **Depth:** `medium` because the design needs care, but the architecture is now pretty clear.
- **Prep:** no `+prep`; we already know the relevant Discord and app behavior from logs/code.

I would split the work into two sensible phases:

1. **Immediate reliability fix:** per-bot reconnect catch-up, bot-scoped replay window, idempotent inserts, tests.  
   Profile: `premium/standard/medium`

2. **Durable inbound queue hardening:** clearer processing states, stale raw/processing sweeper, retry/handled metadata, maybe schema changes.  
   Profile: `premium/standard/medium`, possibly `premium/robust/medium` if we migrate existing production rows or add uniqueness constraints concurrently.

I would not use `super-premium`; this is important production reliability work, but not a new architectural substrate big enough to need two premium models.

`processing` messages with stale `processing_started_at`.\n+   - Re-enqueue or move back to `raw` safely.\n+   - Skip old messages outside a retention window unless explicitly configured.\n+\n+7. **Make catch-up and sweeper cooperate**\n+   - Catch-up inserts or surfaces rows.\n+   - Sweeper handles local unprocessed rows.\n+   - Neither path should duplicate turns for already handled rows.\n+\n+---\n+\n+## Files Expected To Change\n+\n+- `migrations/`\n+  - new migration for handling metadata and any indexes/constraints\n+- `app/services/inbound.py`\n+  - idempotent inbound insert/upsert semantics\n+- `app/services/agentic.py`\n+  - mark triggering messages processing/processed/failed around turn lifecycle\n+- `app/services/scheduled_jobs.py` or a new queue/sweeper module\n+  - periodic recovery of stuck inbound messages\n+- `app/services/discord.py`\n+  - ensure catch-up uses the new idempotent insert and does not bypass state semantics\n+- `app/services/hot_context.py`\n+- `app/services/hot_context_solo.py`\n+  - ensure silent/withheld turns and message states remain auditable in context where relevant\n+- `tests/conftest.py`\n+  - FakePool support for new state transitions\n+- New or updated tests around inbound queue state transitions\n+\n+Planner must inspect existing coalescer behavior before finalizing exact transition points.\n+\n+---\n+\n+## Invariants\n+\n+1. **At-most-once user-visible handling.** A single inbound message should not cause two user-visible replies unless a human explicitly retries with a separate command.\n+2. **At-least-once durable consideration.** A stored inbound message should not remain forever in `raw` or stale `processing` without being retried or marked terminal.\n+3. **Deliberate silence is handled, not ignored.** If the bot intentionally stays silent, the message should still become terminal/auditable.\n+4. **Withheld because newer inbound arrived is handled.** This should be visible as a result, not confused with failure.\n+5. **Provider replay is safe.** Discord catch-up or future provider catch-up can replay recent messages without duplicates.\n+6. **Bot scope is preserved.** Every queue/recovery query must respect `bot_id` and topic/channel scope where applicable.\n+7. **No infinite retry loops.** Permanent errors need terminal marking or bounded retry with audit.\n+\n+---\n+\n+## Edge Cases To Test\n+\n+- App stores inbound row, then crashes before opening a turn. Sweeper later processes it.\n+- App opens a turn, then crashes before completion. Stale `processing` row is recovered.\n+- Model/tool call fails. Message becomes `failed` with error metadata and is retried or left inspectable according to policy.\n+- Outbound send fails after model completes. Message is not incorrectly marked as fully replied unless the send actually succeeded or the turn made an explicit terminal decision.\n+- User sends a newer inbound while an older response is pacing. Older message gets `handling_result='withheld_newer_inbound'`; newer message is processed.\n+- Catch-up replays a processed Discord message; no new turn.\n+- Catch-up replays a raw/stuck Discord message; it gets processed.\n+- Scheduled task/silent turn audit remains accurate.\n+- Multiple bot DMs for the same Discord user do not interfere with each other's queue states.\n+\n+---\n+\n+## Open Questions For The Planner\n+\n+1. Does `messages` already have a uniqueness constraint on `whatsapp_message_id`? If yes, is it bot-scoped and compatible with Discord?\n+2. Is `processing_state='processed'` currently set before or after turn completion? Identify every transition.\n+3. Should failed messages retry automatically, or should only raw/stale-processing messages auto-recover in v1?\n+4. What is the correct retention window for old unprocessed inbound messages: 24h, 7d, or configurable?\n+5. Should coalesced groups mark every triggering message with the same `handled_by_turn_id`, or only the primary trigger? Preferred answer: every triggering message.\n+\n+---\n+\n+## Success Criteria\n+\n+**MUST**\n+\n+- Processing states have documented, enforced semantics.\n+- Inbound messages claimed by a turn are marked `processing` with a timestamp.\n+- Completed turns mark all triggering inbound messages terminal with `handled_by_turn_id`, `handled_at`, and `handling_result`.\n+- Failed turns mark triggering messages `failed` or recoverable according to documented policy.\n+- Stale `processing` messages are recoverable by a sweeper.\n+- Raw messages left behind by downtime are recoverable by a sweeper.\n+- Replaying already handled provider messages does not create duplicate turns or duplicate replies.\n+- Tests cover crash-before-turn, crash-during-turn, duplicate replay, deliberate silence/withheld, and stale processing recovery.\n+\n+**SHOULD**\n+\n+- Queue-state changes are centralized in helper functions.\n+- Sweeper logs recovery counts by bot and state.\n+- `get_bot_actions` or hot context can surface relevant silent/withheld/failed handling results when the user asks why something happened.\n+\n+**INFO**\n+\n+- This sprint does not need to build a user-facing retry UI.\n+- This sprint does not need to rename `whatsapp_message_id`, although future cleanup may want a transport-neutral `external_message_id`.\n+\n*** End Patch\n"
```json
}
```

> AGENT

Yes. This one is a different failure from the earlier reconnect-missed message.

The “Yeah, let's do it please.” message did reach the app and the database:

- Discord message: `1504838075405041825`
- DB message: `ddd562d1-e502-4e88-81f5-a9c7bbc741ee`
- `bot_id`: `hector`
- `processing_state`: `raw`
- It triggered turn `af0673bb-fb12-471e-bc40-bfda95c97146`

But that turn crashed before sending a reply. Railway logs show:

```text
ValueError: invalid UUID 'pending'
DataError: invalid input for query argument $1: 'pending'
/app/app/services/tools/write_tools.py ... in log_event
```

So Hector appears to have tried to call `log_event` with `commitment_id='pending'` instead of creating or finding a real commitment. That invalid value hit the database UUID cast and crashed the turn. Result: no outbound reply, no saved commitment, no completed turn.

On multiple bots: yes, the current approach does allow multiple bots running at the same time. The DB has separate Discord channel rows for `hector`, `mediator`, and `tante_rosi`, and today’s `bot_turns` show all three active. Railway startup logs also showed gateways starting for all three:

```text
discord_gateways_started=hector,mediator,tante_rosi
```

So this was not caused by another bot blocking Hector. It was an isolated Hector turn crash.

The well-engineered fixes are:

1. Route “yes, log it / do it please” confirmations into a `standard`/record path, not `quick_reply`.
2. Make Hector use `list_commitments` then `create_commitment`, not `log_event`, when the user accepts a plan.
3. Harden `log_event` so invalid UUIDs are rejected as tool errors instead of crashing the whole turn.
4. Add the durable retry/handled-message approach from the Megaplan brief so crashed `raw` messages get retried after the code is fixed.

> DEVELOPER

And would our previous plan have caused this to retry? And how could we have prevented this as a matter of like a better system prompt, you know, and why did it hard crash as well? Why could it not recover?

> AGENT

Yes and no.

The **reconnect catch-up plan alone** would probably not have retried this, because this message was not missed by Discord ingestion. It was already in the DB and already triggered a Hector turn.

The **durable inbound queue hardening plan** would have caught it, if implemented as described: the message stayed `processing_state='raw'`, the turn crashed, and there was no outbound response. A sweeper/reconciler looking for stale `raw` or crashed/incomplete turns could retry it after a cooldown, ideally with an attempt counter and idempotency guard.

Why it hard-crashed:

- Hector called `log_event`.
- The tool received `commitment_id='pending'`.
- The DB expects a real UUID.
- `asyncpg` raised a `DataError` while binding the query.
- That exception was not converted into a recoverable tool error.
- The turn runner treated the failed tool call as a fatal turn failure.

So the model made a bad tool call, but the system also let a bad argument cross into SQL and kill the whole turn. That is the part we should fix structurally.

A better system prompt could have reduced the chance by saying something like:

> When the user confirms a proposed routine, do not call `log_event`. First search existing commitments. If no suitable commitment exists, call `create_commitment`. Never invent placeholder IDs such as `pending`; only pass IDs returned by tools.

But prompt alone is not enough. The more robust fix is layered:

1. **Planner/routing:** “yes, do it/log it” after a proposed plan should force a record-capable turn, not `quick_reply`.
2. **Tool schema:** `commitment_id` should be validated as UUID-shaped before the DB call.
3. **Tool behavior:** invalid IDs should return a clean tool error, not crash the turn.
4. **Recovery:** stale `raw` messages and crashed turns should be retried by the queue hardening mechanism.
5. **Prompt:** Hector should be explicitly told the right tool sequence: search existing commitments, create/update commitment, then optionally acknowledge.

So: the previous durable queue plan would have helped recover this after failure, but it would not have prevented the crash. Prevention needs better tool validation plus better Hector/planner instructions.

> DEVELOPER

And, you know, why did it actually crash and how could we prevent that? So I think there's two things. There's one instructing it to better to prevent this happening in the first place. And then the second one is when crashes like this happen, how do we point them back in the right direction? And if we implemented the two previous sprints we described, would this basically have retried? And how long would it have to have retried based on?

> AGENT

I’ll check the two Megaplan briefs we wrote so I don’t overstate what they currently specify about retry timing.

> AGENT

Yes: there are two separate layers here.

**Why It Actually Crashed**
Hector made a bad tool call: he called `log_event` with `commitment_id='pending'`.

That should never be accepted as a real ID. But the tool passed it through to SQL, where the query expected a UUID. `asyncpg` then raised:

```text
invalid UUID 'pending'
```

That exception escaped the tool boundary, so the turn runner marked the whole turn as crashed. It could not recover because the tool failure was treated as a Python/database exception, not as a model-visible tool error like “invalid commitment_id; search or create a commitment first.”

So the model made the mistake, but the system failed to contain it.

**How To Prevent It**
Prompt/instruction prevention:

- Hector should be told: never invent IDs like `pending`, `unknown`, `todo`, etc.
- If the user confirms a plan, first search existing commitments.
- If no matching commitment exists, call `create_commitment`.
- Only call `log_event` against a real commitment ID returned by a tool.
- “Yes, do it / log it / let’s do it” after a proposed routine should be treated as a record/update task, not a quick reply.

System prevention:

- Validate UUID fields before SQL.
- If `commitment_id` is invalid, return a clean tool error.
- Ideally, tool schemas should distinguish “commitment ID required” from “optional link”; the model should not have a loose string field that can reach SQL.
- The turn runner should be able to continue after recoverable tool errors, especially validation errors.

**How To Point It Back In The Right Direction**
When a tool call fails with invalid input, the agent should get a structured error like:

```text
commitment_id must be a UUID returned by list_commitments or create_commitment. Do not invent IDs. Search existing commitments or create one first.
```

Then the model can correct course inside the same turn: call `list_commitments`, then `create_commitment`, then reply.

If the turn still crashes, the queue/sweeper layer should later retry the inbound message with the crash reason available in context.

**Would The Two Previous Sprints Have Retried This?**
The reconnect catch-up sprint: no, not by itself. This message was not missed by Discord. It was already ingested.

The durable inbound queue hardening sprint: yes, if implemented as specified. The message stayed `raw`, had no completed turn, and no outbound reply. The sweeper would find it as stale `raw` or associated with a crashed/incomplete turn, then retry or mark it failed according to policy.

The brief does not currently hard-code exact retry timing. It says “older than a small grace interval,” with retry caps and retention window left for the planner. I’d set v1 like this:

- `raw` older than 1-2 minutes: retry.
- `processing` older than 5-10 minutes: recover/retry.
- retry cap: 3 automatic attempts.
- backoff: roughly 1 min, 5 min, 30 min.
- after cap: mark `failed` with error metadata, do not loop forever.
- retention for auto-recovery: probably 24h initially, configurable later.

For this exact Hector case, a healthy v1 sweeper would likely have retried it within a couple of minutes. But unless we also fix the bad tool validation or prompt/tool routing, it might crash the same way again. The durable queue gives recovery; validation and prompt/tool design prevent repeated failure.

> AGENT

I’d make this a **third Megaplan idea file**, not hand it straight to a sub-agent yet.

Reason: this is a cross-cutting reliability fix, not just a small patch. It touches:

- Hector/agent prompt behavior
- turn routing: `quick_reply` vs `standard`
- tool schema validation
- tool error handling
- turn-runner recovery semantics
- retry behavior from the durable queue sprint

A sub-agent could implement one slice, like “make `log_event` reject invalid UUIDs cleanly,” but the whole fix needs a coherent design so we don’t end up with three partial safeguards that still miss the failure mode.

I’d structure it as a third brief:

`megaplans/tool-error-recovery-and-agent-correction-brief.md`

Scope:

1. Prevent bad tool calls where possible through bot instructions and routing.
2. Validate tool inputs before DB calls.
3. Return structured recoverable tool errors to the model.
4. Let the model retry/correct within the same turn where safe.
5. Mark truly unrecoverable failures cleanly for the durable queue sweeper.
6. Add tests for the exact `commitment_id='pending'` case.

Then once the brief exists, a sub-agent can implement the narrow coding work against that spec. So: Megaplan for the shape, sub-agent for execution after the boundaries are explicit.

> DEVELOPER

And what sequencing makes sense for those three megaplans? Are there any pending or unmade decisions that we need to make?

> AGENT

Sequencing I’d use:

1. **Tool error recovery + agent correction**
   - First because it reduces repeat crashes immediately.
   - It prevents the Hector `commitment_id='pending'` class from turning into a hard crash.
   - It also makes later retries safer, because retrying a broken tool path without this could just replay the same failure.

2. **Discord reconnect catch-up**
   - Second because it fixes provider-level loss: messages sent during gateway reconnect gaps.
   - It is relatively bounded: bot-scoped REST replay, idempotent inserts, no duplicate turns.
   - It does not depend heavily on the durable queue work, but it benefits from the tool layer already being safer.

3. **Durable inbound queue hardening**
   - Third because it is the broadest and most architectural.
   - It should build on the other two: catch-up gets messages into the DB, tool recovery prevents avoidable crashes, then queue hardening handles remaining stuck/crashed/failed cases cleanly.

So the dependency order is:

```text
tool safety -> reconnect replay -> durable queue semantics
```

There are a few decisions still worth making before execution.

For **tool error recovery**:

- Should validation errors allow same-turn correction? I’d say yes, capped at 2 correction attempts per step.
- Which errors are recoverable vs fatal? UUID/placeholder/not-found should be recoverable; database outage should be transient/fail cleanly; permission/policy errors should be terminal.
- Should placeholder ID rejection be global? I’d say yes for all ID fields, not Hector-only.

For **reconnect catch-up**:

- Replay window size: I’d use last 100 messages per bot DM.
- Trigger point: run after every Discord `READY`, including initial connect.
- Duplicate key: use `(bot_id, external Discord message id)`.
- If an existing row is `raw`, should catch-up trigger processing? I’d say yes, but only through shared queue/coalescer logic, not by creating a duplicate turn directly.

For **durable queue hardening**:

- Retry timing: I’d start with `raw > 2 min`, stale `processing > 10 min`, retry backoff `1m / 5m / 30m`, cap 3 attempts.
- Terminal states/results: use explicit `handled_at`, `handled_by_turn_id`, `handling_result`, `processing_error`.
- Failed-message policy: retry only known retryable classes; do not blindly retry permanent validation/policy failures.
- Retention window: I’d start with 24h for automatic recovery, with manual replay possible for older rows.
- Coalesced messages: mark every triggering inbound message handled by the same turn, not only the primary one.

The main open design decision is whether to implement **minimal v1 queue semantics** or the full durable queue state model in one sprint. I’d keep queue hardening v1 tight: add handling metadata, stale recovery, retry cap, and explicit terminal results. Avoid broader renames like `whatsapp_message_id` to `external_message_id` unless the planner proves it is necessary.

> DEVELOPER

And why did it stop? What was wrong with the plan that it stopped? Should we be adding another mega plan? What was wrong with the plan that it stopped?

> AGENT

It stopped because the **second Megaplan’s finalized execution checklist had an impossible dependency order**.

Specifically, it created this order:

1. **T10**: verify “before_execute” user action U1
2. **U1**: apply migration `0040` to prod
3. **T1**: create migration file `0040_bot_scoped_inbound_idempotency.sql`

That is circular. You cannot apply or verify migration `0040` before the sprint has created the migration file. The executor correctly refused to proceed.

So the issue was not the overall design of the reconnect catch-up sprint. The bad part was this planning detail:

- It treated production migration application as a **precondition for executing the code sprint**.
- But creating the migration file is part of the code sprint.
- Therefore the precondition depended on the output of a later task.

Correct shape:

1. Create migration file.
2. Update code to use the new constraint.
3. Add tests.
4. Verify migration text/static behavior locally.
5. After implementation, apply migration during staging/prod deployment.

We do **not** need another Megaplan for this. This is a replan/ordering correction inside the existing second milestone, and I already sent Megaplan back through the planning loop with the note:

> Do not require applying migration 0040 before execute. Create it first; production/staging application belongs in after_execute/deployment notes.

The deeper lesson for future Megaplans is: migrations that are being authored by the sprint should not be listed as `before_execute` user actions. Only external prerequisites that already exist should be `before_execute`. Deployment/application of newly authored migrations belongs after the code diff exists.

> DEVELOPER

And why did it not fix it?

> AGENT

I’ll check the current plan state and latest chain log so I can distinguish “it didn’t fix it” from “it’s still in the re-critique loop.”

> AGENT

It didn’t “fix” instantly because I didn’t patch the already-finalized execution checklist directly. I used `override replan`, which does two things:

1. Adds the correction note.
2. Moves the plan back to `planned` so Megaplan can re-run critique/gate/finalize.

It does **not** immediately rewrite `final.md`, `execution.json`, or clear the old blocked `T10` artifact. That’s why status still shows the old blocked `T10` in progress metadata. That metadata is from the previous failed execute attempt.

Current actual state is different:

- The plan is no longer executing the bad checklist.
- It is back in the planning loop.
- It has already re-run `critique`.
- It is now in `gate`.

So the “not fixed” part is mostly a stale-state/artifact issue until the new finalize step regenerates the execution checklist. If the new finalize still puts migration application before migration creation, then that is a real failure and I’ll directly edit the plan/final checklist or force another replan more explicitly.

The thing I should have done more cleanly: after `override replan`, directly inspect and, if necessary, edit `plan_v1.md` so the ordering constraint is explicit in the plan itself, not only in a note. The note is visible to the next phases, but it’s weaker than changing the plan text.

```json
{
  "cmd": "cat <<'EOF' | PYENV_VERSION=3.11.11 python -m megaplan ticket new \"Chain replan leaves stale blocked execution state and weakly applies ordering fixes\" --tags bug,chain,replan,execution -\nDuring a Veas three-milestone chain, the second milestone (`discord-reconnect-catchup`) stopped with `awaiting_human` because the finalized execution checklist put a before_execute verification task before the task that creates the artifact being verified.\n\nConcrete failure:\n\n- Final checklist had T10 first: verify/apply migration 0040 as a before_execute user action.\n- T1, which creates `migrations/0040_bot_scoped_inbound_idempotency.sql`, depended on T10.\n- The executor correctly blocked because migration 0040 did not exist yet and production still had the old global `UNIQUE (whatsapp_message_id)` constraint.\n\nOperationally confusing behavior after `megaplan override replan`:\n\n- `override replan` added the correction note and moved the plan back to `planned`.\n- Old `execution.json` / `execution_batch_1.json` / progress metadata still reported blocked T10 and 0/10 task progress.\n- Chain status still showed `last_state: awaiting_human` while the plan itself was actively re-critiquing/gating, which made it look as if the replan had not fixed anything.\n- The note was visible to later phases, but the existing `plan_v1.md` text did not itself get rewritten, so the correction relied on the next phases interpreting a note rather than a structurally changed plan.\n\nDesired improvements:\n\n1. Finalizer/executor should not place generated migration application in `before_execute` when the migration file is created by the same sprint. Applying that migration belongs in deployment/after_execute notes; local/static verification can happen during execute.\n2. Dependency validation should catch cycles or impossible before_execute dependencies before execution begins, especially when a user action depends on files produced by later tasks.\n3. `override replan` should make stale blocked execution metadata clearer. Options: mark prior execution artifacts superseded, reset progress display for the new planning loop, or have status distinguish \"previous execute blocked\" from \"current active replan state\".\n4. Replan notes that correct execution ordering should be promoted into the plan artifact or prompt the operator to edit the plan file before continuing, instead of leaving the fix only as an external note.\n5. Chain status should not keep `last_state: awaiting_human` as the dominant user-facing state once the underlying plan has re-entered an active planning/critique/gate step.\n\nThis is a Megaplan tooling issue, not a Veas product issue. It surfaced while running a current-branch chain with `--no-git-refresh --no-push`, but the underlying problem applies to normal chain runs too.\nEOF",
  "workdir": "/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/megaplan",
  "yield_time_ms": 1000,
  "max_output_tokens": 12000
}
```
